I measure things out so it doesn’t all get away from me. My doctor seems to think that’s too tight, but to me it mostly feels orderly.\n\nDoctor: Does your meal planning have anything to do with trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose it does, at least partly. I suppose it’s more about not gaining, if I’m being honest. I don’t have some official number written down or anything—well, I do notice the number, obviously—but it’s that if I’ve eaten a normal dinner, or gone to a restaurant, the next morning I’ll check and if it’s even up a pound I get this awful jolt of panic.

And I know scales fluctuate, I do know that. I used to deal with data, so I understand variation better than most people, probably. But in that moment it feels like I’ve let something slip, like I’ve lost control, and then I start planning how to correct it the next day. So… yes, avoiding gaining weight is part of it.

I might be missing what you mean by habits, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or ideas that you feel compelled to think about over and over?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure. I mean, I do think things through more than once, but I always thought that was just being careful. In operations research, you don’t just accept the first answer—you check the assumptions, run it again, see if there’s some variable you missed.\n\nBut compelled to think something over and over… I don’t know that I’d describe it that way. Maybe I’m not quite understanding what you mean by “ideas.” Are you asking about worries, or more like a phrase getting stuck in your head? Because I don’t think I have anything like that, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with meal planning to maintain a specific weight or avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more about not gaining, if I’m being honest. I don’t have some official number written down or anything—well, I do notice the number, obviously—but it’s that if I’ve eaten a normal dinner, or gone to a restaurant, the next morning I’ll check and if it’s even up a pound I get this awful jolt of panic.\n\nAnd I know scales fluctuate, I do know that. I used to deal with data, so I understand variation better than most people, probably. But in that moment it feels like I’ve let something slip, like I’ve lost control, and then I start planning how to correct it the next day. So… yes, avoiding gaining weight is part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that are not related to food or your weight?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, my mind does wander—I’ll suddenly remember something I needed to put on a list, or I’ll think about whether I locked the back door, but I think that’s just normal, isn’t it? Especially as you get older, you start double-checking things because if you don’t write it down it disappears.\n\nBut recurring intrusive thoughts… not really, at least not outside the eating and weight stuff we were just talking about. I don’t really see it that way. I’ve always liked things orderly—my work was operations research, so measuring and planning doesn’t feel strange to me.\n\nMostly I just keep breakfast simple, like yogurt, and if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and then dinner is measured out so the day doesn’t get away from me. It feels sensible, but apparently she thinks it’s become a concern.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or upset if you can't stick to your meal plan?\n\nPatient: I suppose I get a little unsettled if the plan changes, yes, but I wouldn’t call it dramatic. It’s more that I like the day to have a structure. Breakfast is usually just yogurt, and if I’m busy I may skip lunch—honestly when I was working in operations research, you learn to appreciate systems that don’t have too many variables.\n\nDinner is where I’m more careful, I guess. I measure things out so it doesn’t all get away from me. My doctor seems to think that’s too tight, but to me it mostly feels orderly.\n\nDoctor: Does your meal planning have anything to do with trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose it does, at least partly. I don’t like saying it that way because it sounds vain or irrational, and I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nBut if I’ve had a normal dinner, or especially if I eat out somewhere—restaurants always use so much oil, you know, and the portions are never consistent—then if the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I feel this sharp panic. I’d like to say it’s the routine, because that sounds more sensible, doesn’t it? I’ve always been someone who likes a plan. Even when I was working, if one number was off in a model, it could throw the whole thing, and I suppose I still think that way at home.

Like I’ve lost control somehow, and then I feel I have to correct it the next day. So yes, I think gaining weight is what frightens me most, even if I dress it up as “keeping to the routine.”

When I was working, everything had a process, and I suppose some of that sticks with you.\n\nBut compelled, like I have to do it in a particular way or something terrible will happen… no, I don’t think I have anything like that. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to measure out food and stick to your meal plan is more about keeping things orderly and controlled, or about preventing weight gain?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I tend to tell myself it’s about order. That’s sort of why I came in, really—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning had gotten too tight.\n\nTo me it just feels practical: breakfast is usually yogurt, then if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and dinner is measured out so the day doesn’t get away from me. I’ve always liked systems—when I was working, everything had to be organized or you’d miss something. But… yes, I can see that keeping it that controlled probably also has to do with not gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not following your meal plan, what is it that you fear most: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s the routine, because that sounds more sensible, doesn’t it? I’ve always been someone who likes a plan. Even when I was working, if one number was off in a model, it could throw the whole thing, and I suppose I still think that way at home.\n\nBut if I’m being honest… it’s the weight. If I have a normal dinner, or we go to a restaurant and I can’t really measure things properly, and then the scale is up even a pound, I get this sharp sort of panic. So yes, I think gaining weight is what frightens me most, even if I dress it up as “keeping to the routine.”\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you imagine yourself at your ideal weight, do you think you would still feel the need to measure out your food so carefully?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I suppose… I probably would, yes. Though I’m not sure I think of it as “ideal weight,” exactly. That phrase makes me think of charts at the doctor’s office, and those never seemed very personal, did they? I just think of it as yogurt for breakfast, maybe no lunch if I’m busy, and then a very measured dinner so things don’t get away from me. It feels sensible to me, though I suppose the fact that it feels necessary is what worries everyone.

But… yes, I can see that keeping it that controlled probably also has to do with not gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not following your meal plan, what is it that you fear most: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s the routine, because that sounds more sensible, doesn’t it? I’ve always been someone who likes a plan. Even when I was working, if one number was off in a model, it could throw the whole thing, and I suppose I still think that way at home.\n\nBut if I’m being honest… it’s the weight. If I have a normal dinner, or we go to a restaurant and I can’t really measure things properly, and then the scale is up even a pound, I get this sharp sort of panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow, and then I feel I have to correct it the next day. So yes, I think gaining weight is what frightens me most, even if I dress it up as “keeping to the routine.”\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself at your ideal weight, do you think you would still feel the need to measure out your food so carefully?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I probably would, yes. Though I’m not sure I think of it as “ideal weight,” exactly. That phrase makes me think of charts at the doctor’s office, and those never seemed very personal, did they? When I was doing operations work, we always had targets and ranges, but real life is messier than a spreadsheet.\n\nBut yes, the measuring—I think I’d still do it, because to me it feels like keeping the day orderly. That’s really why I came in, because my doctor thought my meal planning had gotten too tight. I just think of it as yogurt for breakfast, maybe no lunch if I’m busy, and then a very measured dinner so things don’t get away from me. It feels sensible to me, though I suppose the fact that it feels necessary is what worries everyone.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still feel anxious if you gained a small amount of weight but were able to maintain your daily routine and structure?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think I would. I mean, I could have the whole day perfectly arranged—breakfast as planned, errands in the right order, dinner measured out—and if the scale were up even a pound, especially after something like a normal dinner or eating out, I’d still feel this sudden panic.

It’s funny, because in my old work we used to say a small variation wasn’t necessarily meaningful—you had to look at the trend, not one data point. But when it’s me, I don’t seem to treat it that way. It feels like I’ve let something slip, and then I start thinking I need to make up for it the next day. So yes, even with the routine intact, the weight change would still upset me.

It feels sensible, but apparently she thinks it’s become a concern.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or upset if you can't stick to your meal plan?\n\nPatient: I suppose I get a little unsettled if the plan changes, yes, but I wouldn’t call it dramatic. It’s more that I like the day to have a structure. I measure things out so it doesn’t all get away from me. My doctor seems to think that’s too tight, but to me it mostly feels orderly.\n\nDoctor: Does your meal planning have anything to do with trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose it does, at least partly. I don’t like saying it that way because it sounds vain or irrational, and I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nBut if I’ve had a normal dinner, or especially if I eat out somewhere—restaurants always use so much oil, you know, and the portions are never consistent—then if the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I feel this sharp panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow. And then I feel like I need to correct it the next day, tighten things back up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other daily routines or habits that you feel compelled to follow very strictly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve always had routines—most people do, don’t they? I like my kitchen put back a certain way and I keep lists, but that’s just… how I’ve always managed things. Nothing is coming to mind the way the food planning does. I might be missing what you mean by habits, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or ideas that you feel compelled to think about over and over?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure. manifestation: If the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner or a restaurant meal, I feel this sharp panic that I’ve lost control and need to correct it the next day.\n- key: S046\n  name: Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance\n  description: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.\n  manifestation: I know I’m an older woman and not trying to look young, but I still judge how well I’m handling myself by whether my waist and hips look contained in my clothes.\n- key: S084\n  name: Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition\n  description: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.\n  manifestation: When my physician talks about my weight being medically unsafe, part of me thinks she’s overstating it because I’m still working, running my errands, and managing my own life.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still keep up with my analysis work and errands, but I’ve mostly stopped joining friends for meals after book club because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 63 / operations_research_analyst / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S043\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you think about not following your meal plan, what is it that you fear most: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s the routine, because that sounds more sensible, doesn’t it? I’ve always been someone who likes a plan. Even when I was working, if one number was off in a model, it could throw the whole thing, and I suppose I still think that way at home.\n\nBut if I’m being honest… it’s the weight. If I have a normal dinner, or we go to a restaurant and I can’t really measure things properly, and then the scale is up even a pound, I get this sharp sort of panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow, and then I feel I have to correct it the next day. So yes, I think gaining weight is what frightens me most, even if I dress it up as “keeping to the routine.”\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself at your ideal weight, do you think you would still feel the need to measure out your food so carefully?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I probably would, yes. Though I’m not sure I think of it as “ideal weight,” exactly. That phrase makes me think of charts at the doctor’s office, and those never seemed very personal, did they? When I was doing operations work, we always had targets and ranges, but real life is messier than a spreadsheet.\n\nBut yes, the measuring—I think I’d still do it, because to me it feels like keeping the day orderly. That’s really why I came in, because my doctor thought my meal planning had gotten too tight. I just think of it as yogurt for breakfast, maybe no lunch if I’m busy, and then a very measured dinner so things don’t get away from me. It feels sensible to me, though I suppose the fact that it feels necessary is what worries everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if you gained a small amount of weight but were able to maintain your daily routine and structure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, I could have the whole day perfectly arranged—breakfast as planned, errands in the right order, dinner measured out—and if the scale were up even a pound, especially after something like a normal dinner or eating out, I’d still feel this sudden panic.\n\nIt’s funny, because in my old work we used to say a small variation wasn’t necessarily meaningful—you had to look at the trend, not one data point. But when it’s me, I don’t seem to treat it that way. It feels like I’ve let something slip, and then I start thinking I need to make up for it the next day. So yes, even with the routine intact, the weight change would still upset me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you could ever stop measuring your food portions if you felt secure that your weight would remain stable?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe… if I really believed that. But I’m not sure I know how to feel secure about it without checking and measuring, if that makes sense.

I mean, the reason I came in is because my doctor thinks my meal planning has gotten too tight. To me it just feels orderly—yogurt for breakfast, then if I’m busy I might not bother with lunch, and dinner is measured so the day doesn’t get away from me. It’s like when I used to keep spreadsheets balanced; once you stop tracking things, you don’t really know where you are.

So I suppose I’d like to say yes, I could stop measuring, but honestly I think it would be very hard. Measuring is sort of how I make myself feel secure in the first place.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my doctor thought I should come talk to someone because she seems worried my meal planning has gotten a little too… tight, I guess. I don’t really see it that way. I’ve always liked things orderly—my work was operations research, so measuring and planning doesn’t feel strange to me.\n\nMostly I just keep breakfast simple, like yogurt, and if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and then dinner is measured out so the day doesn’t get away from me. It feels sensible, but apparently she thinks it’s become a concern.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or upset if you can't stick to your meal plan?\n\nPatient: I suppose I get a little unsettled if the plan changes, yes, but I wouldn’t call it dramatic. It’s more that I like the day to have a structure. Breakfast is usually just yogurt, and if I’m busy I may skip lunch—honestly when I was working in operations research, you learn to appreciate systems that don’t have too many variables.\n\nDinner is where I’m more careful, I guess. I measure things out so it doesn’t all get away from me. My doctor seems to think that’s too tight, but to me it mostly feels orderly.\n\nDoctor: Does your meal planning have anything to do with trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose it does, at least partly. I don’t like saying it that way because it sounds vain or irrational, and I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nBut if I’ve had a normal dinner, or especially if I eat out somewhere—restaurants always use so much oil, you know, and the portions are never consistent—then if the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I feel this sharp panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow. And then I feel like I need to correct it the next day, tighten things back up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other daily routines or habits that you feel compelled to follow very strictly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve always had routines—most people do, don’t they? I like my kitchen put back a certain way and I keep lists, but that’s just… how I’ve always managed things. When I worked, if you didn’t track details, things fell apart, so maybe I carried some of that with me.\n\nBut compelled, strictly… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the way the food planning does. I might be missing what you mean by habits, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or ideas that you feel compelled to think about over and over?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure. I mean, I do think things through more than once, but I always thought that was just being careful. In operations research, you don’t just accept the first answer—you check the assumptions, run it again, see if there’s some variable you missed.\n\nBut compelled to think something over and over… I don’t know that I’d describe it that way. Maybe I’m not quite understanding what you mean by “ideas.” Are you asking about worries, or more like a phrase getting stuck in your head? Because I don’t think I have anything like that, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with meal planning to maintain a specific weight or avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more about not gaining, if I’m being honest. I don’t have some official number written down or anything—well, I do notice the number, obviously—but it’s that if I’ve eaten a normal dinner, or gone to a restaurant, the next morning I’ll check and if it’s even up a pound I get this awful jolt of panic.\n\nAnd I know scales fluctuate, I do know that. I used to deal with data, so I understand variation better than most people, probably. But in that moment it feels like I’ve let something slip, like I’ve lost control, and then I start planning how to correct it the next day. So… yes, avoiding gaining weight is part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that are not related to food or your weight?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, my mind does wander—I’ll suddenly remember something I needed to put on a list, or I’ll think about whether I locked the back door, but I think that’s just normal, isn’t it? Especially as you get older, you start double-checking things because if you don’t write it down it disappears.\n\nBut recurring intrusive thoughts… not really, at least not outside the eating and weight stuff we were just talking about. Nothing that jumps out at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors outside of your meal planning?\n\nPatient: Rituals… outside of meals? I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines, certainly—at my age you sort of develop systems so you don’t spend half the day looking for your keys. I put the mail in the same spot, I make little lists, that sort of thing. When I was working, everything had a process, and I suppose some of that sticks with you.\n\nBut compelled, like I have to do it in a particular way or something terrible will happen… no, I don’t think I have anything like that. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to measure out food and stick to your meal plan is more about keeping things orderly and controlled, or about preventing weight gain?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I tend to tell myself it’s about order. That’s sort of why I came in, really—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning had gotten too tight.\n\nTo me it just feels practical: breakfast is usually yogurt, then if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and dinner is measured out so the day doesn’t get away from me. I’ve always liked systems—when I was working, everything had to be organized or you’d miss something. But… yes, I can see that keeping it that controlled probably also has to do with not gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not following your meal plan, what is it that you fear most: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s the routine, because that sounds more sensible, doesn’t it? I’ve always been someone who likes a plan. Even when I was working, if one number was off in a model, it could throw the whole thing, and I suppose I still think that way at home.\n\nBut if I’m being honest… it’s the weight. If I have a normal dinner, or we go to a restaurant and I can’t really measure things properly, and then the scale is up even a pound, I get this sharp sort of panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow, and then I feel I have to correct it the next day. So yes, I think gaining weight is what frightens me most, even if I dress it up as “keeping to the routine.”\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself at your ideal weight, do you think you would still feel the need to measure out your food so carefully?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I probably would, yes. Though I’m not sure I think of it as “ideal weight,” exactly. That phrase makes me think of charts at the doctor’s office, and those never seemed very personal, did they? When I was doing operations work, we always had targets and ranges, but real life is messier than a spreadsheet.\n\nBut yes, the measuring—I think I’d still do it, because to me it feels like keeping the day orderly. That’s really why I came in, because my doctor thought my meal planning had gotten too tight. I just think of it as yogurt for breakfast, maybe no lunch if I’m busy, and then a very measured dinner so things don’t get away from me. It feels sensible to me, though I suppose the fact that it feels necessary is what worries everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if you gained a small amount of weight but were able to maintain your daily routine and structure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, I could have the whole day perfectly arranged—breakfast as planned, errands in the right order, dinner measured out—and if the scale were up even a pound, especially after something like a normal dinner or eating out, I’d still feel this sudden panic.\n\nIt’s funny, because in my old work we used to say a small variation wasn’t necessarily meaningful—you had to look at the trend, not one data point. But when it’s me, I don’t seem to treat it that way. It feels like I’ve let something slip, and then I start thinking I need to make up for it the next day. So yes, even with the routine intact, the weight change would still upset me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever stop measuring your food portions if you felt secure that your weight would remain stable?\n\nPatient: Maybe… if I really believed that. But I’m not sure I know how to feel secure about it without checking and measuring, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, the reason I came in is because my doctor thinks my meal planning has gotten too tight. To me it just feels orderly—yogurt for breakfast, then if I’m busy I might not bother with lunch, and dinner is measured so the day doesn’t get away from me. It’s like when I used to keep spreadsheets balanced; once you stop tracking things, you don’t really know where you are.\n\nSo I suppose I’d like to say yes, I could stop measuring, but honestly I think it would be very hard. Measuring is sort of how I make myself feel secure in the first place.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I could have the whole day perfectly arranged—breakfast as planned, errands in the right order, dinner measured out—and if the scale were up even a pound, especially after something like a normal dinner or eating out, I’d still feel this sudden panic.\n\nIt’s funny, because in my old work we used to say a small variation wasn’t necessarily meaningful—you had to look at the trend, not one data point. But when it’s me, I don’t seem to treat it that way. It feels like I’ve let something slip, and then I start thinking I need to make up for it the next day. To me it just feels orderly—yogurt for breakfast, then if I’m busy I might not bother with lunch, and dinner is measured so the day doesn’t get away from me. It’s like when I used to keep spreadsheets balanced; once you stop tracking things, you don’t really know where you are.\n\nSo I suppose I’d like to say yes, I could stop measuring, but honestly I think it would be very hard. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more how it makes me feel about myself, though I suppose the actual weight is the number I latch onto. It’s… the number is like the signal that something is wrong.

I know I’m not a young woman trying to fit into some magazine idea—goodness, at my age I’m not trying to look twenty-five again. But I still find myself judging whether I’m handling things properly by how my waist and hips look in my clothes. If they feel contained, if my clothes sit right, then I feel more… in control, I guess.

It’s funny, I used to be very practical about clothing—good seams, washable fabrics, pockets if you could find them, which is nearly impossible in women’s clothes—but now I notice whether things feel snug in those areas, and it affects how I see the whole day. So yes, it’s not just the pounds. It’s what I think they say about me.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my doctor thought I should come talk to someone because she seems worried my meal planning has gotten a little too… tight, I guess. I don’t really see it that way. I’ve always liked things orderly—my work was operations research, so measuring and planning doesn’t feel strange to me.\n\nMostly I just keep breakfast simple, like yogurt, and if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and then dinner is measured out so the day doesn’t get away from me. It feels sensible, but apparently she thinks it’s become a concern.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or upset if you can't stick to your meal plan?\n\nPatient: I suppose I get a little unsettled if the plan changes, yes, but I wouldn’t call it dramatic. It’s more that I like the day to have a structure. Breakfast is usually just yogurt, and if I’m busy I may skip lunch—honestly when I was working in operations research, you learn to appreciate systems that don’t have too many variables.\n\nDinner is where I’m more careful, I guess. I measure things out so it doesn’t all get away from me. My doctor seems to think that’s too tight, but to me it mostly feels orderly.\n\nDoctor: Does your meal planning have anything to do with trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose it does, at least partly. I don’t like saying it that way because it sounds vain or irrational, and I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nBut if I’ve had a normal dinner, or especially if I eat out somewhere—restaurants always use so much oil, you know, and the portions are never consistent—then if the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I feel this sharp panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow. And then I feel like I need to correct it the next day, tighten things back up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other daily routines or habits that you feel compelled to follow very strictly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve always had routines—most people do, don’t they? I like my kitchen put back a certain way and I keep lists, but that’s just… how I’ve always managed things. When I worked, if you didn’t track details, things fell apart, so maybe I carried some of that with me.\n\nBut compelled, strictly… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the way the food planning does. I might be missing what you mean by habits, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or ideas that you feel compelled to think about over and over?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure. I mean, I do think things through more than once, but I always thought that was just being careful. In operations research, you don’t just accept the first answer—you check the assumptions, run it again, see if there’s some variable you missed.\n\nBut compelled to think something over and over… I don’t know that I’d describe it that way. Maybe I’m not quite understanding what you mean by “ideas.” Are you asking about worries, or more like a phrase getting stuck in your head? Because I don’t think I have anything like that, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with meal planning to maintain a specific weight or avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more about not gaining, if I’m being honest. I don’t have some official number written down or anything—well, I do notice the number, obviously—but it’s that if I’ve eaten a normal dinner, or gone to a restaurant, the next morning I’ll check and if it’s even up a pound I get this awful jolt of panic.\n\nAnd I know scales fluctuate, I do know that. I used to deal with data, so I understand variation better than most people, probably. But in that moment it feels like I’ve let something slip, like I’ve lost control, and then I start planning how to correct it the next day. So… yes, avoiding gaining weight is part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that are not related to food or your weight?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, my mind does wander—I’ll suddenly remember something I needed to put on a list, or I’ll think about whether I locked the back door, but I think that’s just normal, isn’t it? Especially as you get older, you start double-checking things because if you don’t write it down it disappears.\n\nBut recurring intrusive thoughts… not really, at least not outside the eating and weight stuff we were just talking about. Nothing that jumps out at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors outside of your meal planning?\n\nPatient: Rituals… outside of meals? I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines, certainly—at my age you sort of develop systems so you don’t spend half the day looking for your keys. I put the mail in the same spot, I make little lists, that sort of thing. When I was working, everything had a process, and I suppose some of that sticks with you.\n\nBut compelled, like I have to do it in a particular way or something terrible will happen… no, I don’t think I have anything like that. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to measure out food and stick to your meal plan is more about keeping things orderly and controlled, or about preventing weight gain?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I tend to tell myself it’s about order. That’s sort of why I came in, really—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning had gotten too tight.\n\nTo me it just feels practical: breakfast is usually yogurt, then if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and dinner is measured out so the day doesn’t get away from me. I’ve always liked systems—when I was working, everything had to be organized or you’d miss something. But… yes, I can see that keeping it that controlled probably also has to do with not gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not following your meal plan, what is it that you fear most: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s the routine, because that sounds more sensible, doesn’t it? I’ve always been someone who likes a plan. Even when I was working, if one number was off in a model, it could throw the whole thing, and I suppose I still think that way at home.\n\nBut if I’m being honest… it’s the weight. If I have a normal dinner, or we go to a restaurant and I can’t really measure things properly, and then the scale is up even a pound, I get this sharp sort of panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow, and then I feel I have to correct it the next day. So yes, I think gaining weight is what frightens me most, even if I dress it up as “keeping to the routine.”\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself at your ideal weight, do you think you would still feel the need to measure out your food so carefully?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I probably would, yes. Though I’m not sure I think of it as “ideal weight,” exactly. That phrase makes me think of charts at the doctor’s office, and those never seemed very personal, did they? When I was doing operations work, we always had targets and ranges, but real life is messier than a spreadsheet.\n\nBut yes, the measuring—I think I’d still do it, because to me it feels like keeping the day orderly. I just think of it as yogurt for breakfast, maybe no lunch if I’m busy, and then a very measured dinner so things don’t get away from me. It feels sensible to me, though I suppose the fact that it feels necessary is what worries everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if you gained a small amount of weight but were able to maintain your daily routine and structure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. But when it’s me, I don’t seem to treat it that way. It feels like I’ve let something slip, and then I start thinking I need to make up for it the next day. So yes, even with the routine intact, the weight change would still upset me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever stop measuring your food portions if you felt secure that your weight would remain stable?\n\nPatient: Maybe… if I really believed that. Maybe I’m not quite understanding what you mean by “ideas.” Are you asking about worries, or more like a phrase getting stuck in your head? Because I don’t think I have anything like that, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with meal planning to maintain a specific weight or avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more about not gaining, if I’m being honest. I don’t have some official number written down or anything—well, I do notice the number, obviously—but it’s that if I’ve eaten a normal dinner, or gone to a restaurant, the next morning I’ll check and if it’s even up a pound I get this awful jolt of panic.\n\nAnd I know scales fluctuate, I do know that. I used to deal with data, so I understand variation better than most people, probably. But in that moment it feels like I’ve let something slip, like I’ve lost control, and then I start planning how to correct it the next day. So… yes, avoiding gaining weight is part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that are not related to food or your weight?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, my mind does wander—I’ll suddenly remember something I needed to put on a list, or I’ll think about whether I locked the back door, but I think that’s just normal, isn’t it? Especially as you get older, you start double-checking things because if you don’t write it down it disappears.\n\nBut recurring intrusive thoughts… not really, at least not outside the eating and weight stuff we were just talking about. Nothing that jumps out at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors outside of your meal planning?\n\nPatient: Rituals… outside of meals? I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines, certainly—at my age you sort of develop systems so you don’t spend half the day looking for your keys. I put the mail in the same spot, I make little lists, that sort of thing. When I was working, everything had a process, and I suppose some of that sticks with you.\n\nBut compelled, like I have to do it in a particular way or something terrible will happen… no, I don’t think I have anything like that. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to measure out food and stick to your meal plan is more about keeping things orderly and controlled, or about preventing weight gain?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I tend to tell myself it’s about order. That’s sort of why I came in, really—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning had gotten too tight.\n\nTo me it just feels practical: breakfast is usually yogurt, then if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and dinner is measured out so the day doesn’t get away from me. I’ve always liked systems—when I was working, everything had to be organized or you’d miss something. But… yes, I can see that keeping it that controlled probably also has to do with not gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not following your meal plan, what is it that you fear most: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s the routine, because that sounds more sensible, doesn’t it? I’ve always been someone who likes a plan. Even when I was working, if one number was off in a model, it could throw the whole thing, and I suppose I still think that way at home.\n\nBut if I’m being honest… it’s the weight. If I have a normal dinner, or we go to a restaurant and I can’t really measure things properly, and then the scale is up even a pound, I get this sharp sort of panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow, and then I feel I have to correct it the next day. So yes, I think gaining weight is what frightens me most, even if I dress it up as “keeping to the routine.”\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself at your ideal weight, do you think you would still feel the need to measure out your food so carefully?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I probably would, yes. Though I’m not sure I think of it as “ideal weight,” exactly. That phrase makes me think of charts at the doctor’s office, and those never seemed very personal, did they? When I was doing operations work, we always had targets and ranges, but real life is messier than a spreadsheet.\n\nBut yes, the measuring—I think I’d still do it, because to me it feels like keeping the day orderly. That’s really why I came in, because my doctor thought my meal planning had gotten too tight. I just think of it as yogurt for breakfast, maybe no lunch if I’m busy, and then a very measured dinner so things don’t get away from me. It feels sensible to me, though I suppose the fact that it feels necessary is what worries everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if you gained a small amount of weight but were able to maintain your daily routine and structure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, I could have the whole day perfectly arranged—breakfast as planned, errands in the right order, dinner measured out—and if the scale were up even a pound, especially after something like a normal dinner or eating out, I’d still feel this sudden panic.\n\nIt’s funny, because in my old work we used to say a small variation wasn’t necessarily meaningful—you had to look at the trend, not one data point. But when it’s me, I don’t seem to treat it that way. It feels like I’ve let something slip, and then I start thinking I need to make up for it the next day. So yes, even with the routine intact, the weight change would still upset me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever stop measuring your food portions if you felt secure that your weight would remain stable?\n\nPatient: Maybe… if I really believed that. But I’m not sure I know how to feel secure about it without checking and measuring, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, the reason I came in is because my doctor thinks my meal planning has gotten too tight. To me it just feels orderly—yogurt for breakfast, then if I’m busy I might not bother with lunch, and dinner is measured so the day doesn’t get away from me. It’s like when I used to keep spreadsheets balanced; once you stop tracking things, you don’t really know where you are.\n\nSo I suppose I’d like to say yes, I could stop measuring, but honestly I think it would be very hard. Measuring is sort of how I make myself feel secure in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about weight gain more about the actual weight or how it makes you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more how it makes me feel about myself, though I suppose the actual weight is the number I latch onto. It’s… the number is like the signal that something is wrong.\n\nI know I’m not a young woman trying to fit into some magazine idea—goodness, at my age I’m not trying to look twenty-five again. But I still find myself judging whether I’m handling things properly by how my waist and hips look in my clothes. If they feel contained, if my clothes sit right, then I feel more… in control, I guess.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be very practical about clothing—good seams, washable fabrics, pockets if you could find them, which is nearly impossible in women’s clothes—but now I notice whether things feel snug in those areas, and it affects how I see the whole day. So yes, it’s not just the pounds. It’s what I think they say about me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I suppose it’s a little of both, but I think I tell myself it’s about keeping life orderly. My doctor is the one who seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels… sensible, I guess.

It reminds me of scheduling work—though, I suppose that’s not exactly the same thing, is it? But there’s a comfort in knowing what’s coming and not having to make too many decisions.

So yes, preventing weight gain is part of it, but the way it feels to me day-to-day is more like keeping everything in its proper place.

But when it’s me, I don’t seem to treat it that way. It feels like I’ve let something slip, and then I start thinking I need to make up for it the next day. So yes, even with the routine intact, the weight change would still upset me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever stop measuring your food portions if you felt secure that your weight would remain stable?\n\nPatient: Maybe… if I really believed that. But I’m not sure I know how to feel secure about it without checking and measuring, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, the reason I came in is because my doctor thinks my meal planning has gotten too tight. To me it just feels orderly—yogurt for breakfast, then if I’m busy I might not bother with lunch, and dinner is measured so the day doesn’t get away from me. It’s like when I used to keep spreadsheets balanced; once you stop tracking things, you don’t really know where you are.\n\nSo I suppose I’d like to say yes, I could stop measuring, but honestly I think it would be very hard. Measuring is sort of how I make myself feel secure in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about weight gain more about the actual weight or how it makes you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more how it makes me feel about myself, though I suppose the actual weight is the number I latch onto. It’s… the number is like the signal that something is wrong.\n\nI know I’m not a young woman trying to fit into some magazine idea—goodness, at my age I’m not trying to look twenty-five again. But I still find myself judging whether I’m handling things properly by how my waist and hips look in my clothes. If they feel contained, if my clothes sit right, then I feel more… in control, I guess.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be very practical about clothing—good seams, washable fabrics, pockets if you could find them, which is nearly impossible in women’s clothes—but now I notice whether things feel snug in those areas, and it affects how I see the whole day. So yes, it’s not just the pounds. It’s what I think they say about me.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to control your food intake more about preventing weight gain or about feeling in control of your life in general?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a little of both, but I think I tell myself it’s about keeping life orderly. My doctor is the one who seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels… sensible, I guess.\n\nI’ll have yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I may skip lunch without really thinking much of it, and then dinner is very measured so the day doesn’t sort of spread out all over the place. It reminds me of scheduling work—though, I suppose that’s not exactly the same thing, is it? But there’s a comfort in knowing what’s coming and not having to make too many decisions.\n\nSo yes, preventing weight gain is part of it, but the way it feels to me day-to-day is more like keeping everything in its proper place.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I used to deal with data, so I understand variation better than most people, probably. I’ve always liked systems—when I was working, everything had to be organized or you’d miss something. But… yes, I can see that keeping it that controlled probably also has to do with not gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not following your meal plan, what is it that you fear most: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s the routine, because that sounds more sensible, doesn’t it? Like I’ve lost control somehow, and then I feel I have to correct it the next day. When I was doing operations work, we always had targets and ranges, but real life is messier than a spreadsheet.\n\nBut yes, the measuring—I think I’d still do it, because to me it feels like keeping the day orderly. That’s really why I came in, because my doctor thought my meal planning had gotten too tight. I just think of it as yogurt for breakfast, maybe no lunch if I’m busy, and then a very measured dinner so things don’t get away from me. It feels sensible to me, though I suppose the fact that it feels necessary is what worries everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if you gained a small amount of weight but were able to maintain your daily routine and structure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. If I truly didn’t have to worry about my weight at all? Maybe I could relax it some, but I’m not sure I know how to separate the two.

I mean, I came in because my doctor thinks my meal planning has gotten too tight, though to me it feels more like keeping things orderly. So I suppose if weight weren’t a worry, maybe the measuring would feel less necessary. But part of me would still want the structure. It’s hard to imagine just… eating without a plan.

It feels sensible, but apparently she thinks it’s become a concern.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or upset if you can't stick to your meal plan?\n\nPatient: I suppose I get a little unsettled if the plan changes, yes, but I wouldn’t call it dramatic. It’s more that I like the day to have a structure. Breakfast is usually just yogurt, and if I’m busy I may skip lunch—honestly when I was working in operations research, you learn to appreciate systems that don’t have too many variables.\n\nDinner is where I’m more careful, I guess. I measure things out so it doesn’t all get away from me. My doctor seems to think that’s too tight, but to me it mostly feels orderly.\n\nDoctor: Does your meal planning have anything to do with trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose it does, at least partly. I don’t like saying it that way because it sounds vain or irrational, and I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nBut if I’ve had a normal dinner, or especially if I eat out somewhere—restaurants always use so much oil, you know, and the portions are never consistent—then if the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I feel this sharp panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow. And then I feel like I need to correct it the next day, tighten things back up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other daily routines or habits that you feel compelled to follow very strictly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve always had routines—most people do, don’t they? I like my kitchen put back a certain way and I keep lists, but that’s just… how I’ve always managed things. When I worked, if you didn’t track details, things fell apart, so maybe I carried some of that with me.\n\nBut compelled, strictly… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the way the food planning does. I might be missing what you mean by habits, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or ideas that you feel compelled to think about over and over?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure. I mean, I do think things through more than once, but I always thought that was just being careful. In operations research, you don’t just accept the first answer—you check the assumptions, run it again, see if there’s some variable you missed.\n\nBut compelled to think something over and over… I don’t know that I’d describe it that way. Maybe I’m not quite understanding what you mean by “ideas.” Are you asking about worries, or more like a phrase getting stuck in your head? Because I don’t think I have anything like that, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with meal planning to maintain a specific weight or avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more about not gaining, if I’m being honest. I don’t have some official number written down or anything—well, I do notice the number, obviously—but it’s that if I’ve eaten a normal dinner, or gone to a restaurant, the next morning I’ll check and if it’s even up a pound I get this awful jolt of panic.\n\nAnd I know scales fluctuate, I do know that. I used to deal with data, so I understand variation better than most people, probably. But in that moment it feels like I’ve let something slip, like I’ve lost control, and then I start planning how to correct it the next day. So… yes, avoiding gaining weight is part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that are not related to food or your weight?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, my mind does wander—I’ll suddenly remember something I needed to put on a list, or I’ll think about whether I locked the back door, but I think that’s just normal, isn’t it? Especially as you get older, you start double-checking things because if you don’t write it down it disappears.\n\nBut recurring intrusive thoughts… not really, at least not outside the eating and weight stuff we were just talking about. Nothing that jumps out at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors outside of your meal planning?\n\nPatient: Rituals… outside of meals? I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines, certainly—at my age you sort of develop systems so you don’t spend half the day looking for your keys. I put the mail in the same spot, I make little lists, that sort of thing. When I was working, everything had a process, and I suppose some of that sticks with you.\n\nBut compelled, like I have to do it in a particular way or something terrible will happen… no, I don’t think I have anything like that. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to measure out food and stick to your meal plan is more about keeping things orderly and controlled, or about preventing weight gain?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I tend to tell myself it’s about order. That’s sort of why I came in, really—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning had gotten too tight.\n\nTo me it just feels practical: breakfast is usually yogurt, then if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and dinner is measured out so the day doesn’t get away from me. I’ve always liked systems—when I was working, everything had to be organized or you’d miss something. But… yes, I can see that keeping it that controlled probably also has to do with not gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not following your meal plan, what is it that you fear most: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s the routine, because that sounds more sensible, doesn’t it? I’ve always been someone who likes a plan. Even when I was working, if one number was off in a model, it could throw the whole thing, and I suppose I still think that way at home.\n\nBut if I’m being honest… it’s the weight. If I have a normal dinner, or we go to a restaurant and I can’t really measure things properly, and then the scale is up even a pound, I get this sharp sort of panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow, and then I feel I have to correct it the next day. So yes, I think gaining weight is what frightens me most, even if I dress it up as “keeping to the routine.”\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself at your ideal weight, do you think you would still feel the need to measure out your food so carefully?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I probably would, yes. Though I’m not sure I think of it as “ideal weight,” exactly. That phrase makes me think of charts at the doctor’s office, and those never seemed very personal, did they? When I was doing operations work, we always had targets and ranges, but real life is messier than a spreadsheet.\n\nBut yes, the measuring—I think I’d still do it, because to me it feels like keeping the day orderly. That’s really why I came in, because my doctor thought my meal planning had gotten too tight. I just think of it as yogurt for breakfast, maybe no lunch if I’m busy, and then a very measured dinner so things don’t get away from me. It feels sensible to me, though I suppose the fact that it feels necessary is what worries everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if you gained a small amount of weight but were able to maintain your daily routine and structure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, I could have the whole day perfectly arranged—breakfast as planned, errands in the right order, dinner measured out—and if the scale were up even a pound, especially after something like a normal dinner or eating out, I’d still feel this sudden panic.\n\nIt’s funny, because in my old work we used to say a small variation wasn’t necessarily meaningful—you had to look at the trend, not one data point. But when it’s me, I don’t seem to treat it that way. It feels like I’ve let something slip, and then I start thinking I need to make up for it the next day. So yes, even with the routine intact, the weight change would still upset me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever stop measuring your food portions if you felt secure that your weight would remain stable?\n\nPatient: Maybe… if I really believed that. But I’m not sure I know how to feel secure about it without checking and measuring, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, the reason I came in is because my doctor thinks my meal planning has gotten too tight. To me it just feels orderly—yogurt for breakfast, then if I’m busy I might not bother with lunch, and dinner is measured so the day doesn’t get away from me. It’s like when I used to keep spreadsheets balanced; once you stop tracking things, you don’t really know where you are.\n\nSo I suppose I’d like to say yes, I could stop measuring, but honestly I think it would be very hard. Measuring is sort of how I make myself feel secure in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about weight gain more about the actual weight or how it makes you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more how it makes me feel about myself, though I suppose the actual weight is the number I latch onto. It’s… the number is like the signal that something is wrong.\n\nI know I’m not a young woman trying to fit into some magazine idea—goodness, at my age I’m not trying to look twenty-five again. But I still find myself judging whether I’m handling things properly by how my waist and hips look in my clothes. If they feel contained, if my clothes sit right, then I feel more… in control, I guess.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be very practical about clothing—good seams, washable fabrics, pockets if you could find them, which is nearly impossible in women’s clothes—but now I notice whether things feel snug in those areas, and it affects how I see the whole day. So yes, it’s not just the pounds. It’s what I think they say about me.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to control your food intake more about preventing weight gain or about feeling in control of your life in general?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a little of both, but I think I tell myself it’s about keeping life orderly. My doctor is the one who seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels… sensible, I guess.\n\nI’ll have yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I may skip lunch without really thinking much of it, and then dinner is very measured so the day doesn’t sort of spread out all over the place. It reminds me of scheduling work—though, I suppose that’s not exactly the same thing, is it? But there’s a comfort in knowing what’s coming and not having to make too many decisions.\n\nSo yes, preventing weight gain is part of it, but the way it feels to me day-to-day is more like keeping everything in its proper place.\n\nDoctor: Is your strict meal planning something you think you could relax if you didn't have to worry about your weight at all?\n\nPatient: If I truly didn’t have to worry about my weight at all? Maybe I could relax it some, but I’m not sure I know how to separate the two.\n\nI mean, I came in because my doctor thinks my meal planning has gotten too tight, though to me it feels more like keeping things orderly. Yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I might just not stop for lunch—though that happens with paperwork too, you know, you look up and the afternoon’s gone—and then dinner is measured so the day doesn’t get away from me.\n\nSo I suppose if weight weren’t a worry, maybe the measuring would feel less necessary. But part of me would still want the structure. It’s hard to imagine just… eating without a plan.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about weight gain more about the actual weight or how it makes you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more how it makes me feel about myself, though I suppose the actual weight is the number I latch onto. It’s… the number is like the signal that something is wrong.\n\nI know I’m not a young woman trying to fit into some magazine idea—goodness, at my age I’m not trying to look twenty-five again. But I still find myself judging whether I’m handling things properly by how my waist and hips look in my clothes. If they feel contained, if my clothes sit right, then I feel more… in control, I guess.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be very practical about clothing—good seams, washable fabrics, pockets if you could find them, which is nearly impossible in women’s clothes—but now I notice whether things feel snug in those areas, and it affects how I see the whole day. So yes, it’s not just the pounds. It’s what I think they say about me.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to control your food intake more about preventing weight gain or about feeling in control of your life in general?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a little of both, but I think I tell myself it’s about keeping life orderly. My doctor is the one who seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels… sensible, I guess.\n\nI’ll have yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I may skip lunch without really thinking much of it, and then dinner is very measured so the day doesn’t sort of spread out all over the place. It reminds me of scheduling work—though, I suppose that’s not exactly the same thing, is it? But there’s a comfort in knowing what’s coming and not having to make too many decisions.\n\nSo yes, preventing weight gain is part of it, but the way it feels to me day-to-day is more like keeping everything in its proper place.\n\nDoctor: Is your strict meal planning something you think you could relax if you didn't have to worry about your weight at all?\n\nPatient: If I truly didn’t have to worry about my weight at all? Maybe I could relax it some, but I’m not sure I know how to separate the two.\n\nI mean, I came in because my doctor thinks my meal planning has gotten too tight, though to me it feels more like keeping things orderly. Yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I might just not stop for lunch—though that happens with paperwork too, you know, you look up and the afternoon’s gone—and then dinner is measured so the day doesn’t get away from me.\n\nSo I suppose if weight weren’t a worry, maybe the measuring would feel less necessary. But part of me would still want the structure. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… probably yes, at least some. If I were at what I thought was the right weight, I’d still want the routine, because that’s what keeps the day from feeling loose around the edges.

I know my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels practical—yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and then dinner is very measured. It’s almost like how I used to organize reports at work—everything in its place, nothing left dangling. Though I suppose food isn’t exactly a spreadsheet, is it?

So yes, even at an ideal weight, I think I’d still feel pulled to keep it controlled. Maybe not as nervously, but I’d still want it orderly.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my doctor thought I should come talk to someone because she seems worried my meal planning has gotten a little too… tight, I guess. I don’t really see it that way. I’ve always liked things orderly—my work was operations research, so measuring and planning doesn’t feel strange to me.\n\nMostly I just keep breakfast simple, like yogurt, and if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and then dinner is measured out so the day doesn’t get away from me. It feels sensible, but apparently she thinks it’s become a concern.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or upset if you can't stick to your meal plan?\n\nPatient: I suppose I get a little unsettled if the plan changes, yes, but I wouldn’t call it dramatic. It’s more that I like the day to have a structure. Breakfast is usually just yogurt, and if I’m busy I may skip lunch—honestly when I was working in operations research, you learn to appreciate systems that don’t have too many variables.\n\nDinner is where I’m more careful, I guess. I measure things out so it doesn’t all get away from me. My doctor seems to think that’s too tight, but to me it mostly feels orderly.\n\nDoctor: Does your meal planning have anything to do with trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose it does, at least partly. I don’t like saying it that way because it sounds vain or irrational, and I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nBut if I’ve had a normal dinner, or especially if I eat out somewhere—restaurants always use so much oil, you know, and the portions are never consistent—then if the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I feel this sharp panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow. And then I feel like I need to correct it the next day, tighten things back up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other daily routines or habits that you feel compelled to follow very strictly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve always had routines—most people do, don’t they? I like my kitchen put back a certain way and I keep lists, but that’s just… how I’ve always managed things. When I worked, if you didn’t track details, things fell apart, so maybe I carried some of that with me.\n\nBut compelled, strictly… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the way the food planning does. I might be missing what you mean by habits, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or ideas that you feel compelled to think about over and over?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure. I mean, I do think things through more than once, but I always thought that was just being careful. In operations research, you don’t just accept the first answer—you check the assumptions, run it again, see if there’s some variable you missed.\n\nBut compelled to think something over and over… I don’t know that I’d describe it that way. Maybe I’m not quite understanding what you mean by “ideas.” Are you asking about worries, or more like a phrase getting stuck in your head? Because I don’t think I have anything like that, not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal with meal planning to maintain a specific weight or avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more about not gaining, if I’m being honest. I don’t have some official number written down or anything—well, I do notice the number, obviously—but it’s that if I’ve eaten a normal dinner, or gone to a restaurant, the next morning I’ll check and if it’s even up a pound I get this awful jolt of panic.\n\nAnd I know scales fluctuate, I do know that. I used to deal with data, so I understand variation better than most people, probably. But in that moment it feels like I’ve let something slip, like I’ve lost control, and then I start planning how to correct it the next day. So… yes, avoiding gaining weight is part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that are not related to food or your weight?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, my mind does wander—I’ll suddenly remember something I needed to put on a list, or I’ll think about whether I locked the back door, but I think that’s just normal, isn’t it? Especially as you get older, you start double-checking things because if you don’t write it down it disappears.\n\nBut recurring intrusive thoughts… not really, at least not outside the eating and weight stuff we were just talking about. Nothing that jumps out at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors outside of your meal planning?\n\nPatient: Rituals… outside of meals? I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines, certainly—at my age you sort of develop systems so you don’t spend half the day looking for your keys. I put the mail in the same spot, I make little lists, that sort of thing. When I was working, everything had a process, and I suppose some of that sticks with you.\n\nBut compelled, like I have to do it in a particular way or something terrible will happen… no, I don’t think I have anything like that. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to measure out food and stick to your meal plan is more about keeping things orderly and controlled, or about preventing weight gain?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a bit of both, though I tend to tell myself it’s about order. That’s sort of why I came in, really—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning had gotten too tight.\n\nTo me it just feels practical: breakfast is usually yogurt, then if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and dinner is measured out so the day doesn’t get away from me. I’ve always liked systems—when I was working, everything had to be organized or you’d miss something. But… yes, I can see that keeping it that controlled probably also has to do with not gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not following your meal plan, what is it that you fear most: gaining weight or losing control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s the routine, because that sounds more sensible, doesn’t it? I’ve always been someone who likes a plan. Even when I was working, if one number was off in a model, it could throw the whole thing, and I suppose I still think that way at home.\n\nBut if I’m being honest… it’s the weight. If I have a normal dinner, or we go to a restaurant and I can’t really measure things properly, and then the scale is up even a pound, I get this sharp sort of panic. Like I’ve lost control somehow, and then I feel I have to correct it the next day. So yes, I think gaining weight is what frightens me most, even if I dress it up as “keeping to the routine.”\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself at your ideal weight, do you think you would still feel the need to measure out your food so carefully?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I probably would, yes. Though I’m not sure I think of it as “ideal weight,” exactly. That phrase makes me think of charts at the doctor’s office, and those never seemed very personal, did they? When I was doing operations work, we always had targets and ranges, but real life is messier than a spreadsheet.\n\nBut yes, the measuring—I think I’d still do it, because to me it feels like keeping the day orderly. That’s really why I came in, because my doctor thought my meal planning had gotten too tight. I just think of it as yogurt for breakfast, maybe no lunch if I’m busy, and then a very measured dinner so things don’t get away from me. It feels sensible to me, though I suppose the fact that it feels necessary is what worries everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious if you gained a small amount of weight but were able to maintain your daily routine and structure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, I could have the whole day perfectly arranged—breakfast as planned, errands in the right order, dinner measured out—and if the scale were up even a pound, especially after something like a normal dinner or eating out, I’d still feel this sudden panic.\n\nIt’s funny, because in my old work we used to say a small variation wasn’t necessarily meaningful—you had to look at the trend, not one data point. But when it’s me, I don’t seem to treat it that way. It feels like I’ve let something slip, and then I start thinking I need to make up for it the next day. So yes, even with the routine intact, the weight change would still upset me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever stop measuring your food portions if you felt secure that your weight would remain stable?\n\nPatient: Maybe… if I really believed that. But I’m not sure I know how to feel secure about it without checking and measuring, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, the reason I came in is because my doctor thinks my meal planning has gotten too tight. To me it just feels orderly—yogurt for breakfast, then if I’m busy I might not bother with lunch, and dinner is measured so the day doesn’t get away from me. It’s like when I used to keep spreadsheets balanced; once you stop tracking things, you don’t really know where you are.\n\nSo I suppose I’d like to say yes, I could stop measuring, but honestly I think it would be very hard. Measuring is sort of how I make myself feel secure in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about weight gain more about the actual weight or how it makes you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more how it makes me feel about myself, though I suppose the actual weight is the number I latch onto. It’s… the number is like the signal that something is wrong.\n\nI know I’m not a young woman trying to fit into some magazine idea—goodness, at my age I’m not trying to look twenty-five again. But I still find myself judging whether I’m handling things properly by how my waist and hips look in my clothes. If they feel contained, if my clothes sit right, then I feel more… in control, I guess.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be very practical about clothing—good seams, washable fabrics, pockets if you could find them, which is nearly impossible in women’s clothes—but now I notice whether things feel snug in those areas, and it affects how I see the whole day. So yes, it’s not just the pounds. It’s what I think they say about me.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to control your food intake more about preventing weight gain or about feeling in control of your life in general?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s a little of both, but I think I tell myself it’s about keeping life orderly. My doctor is the one who seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels… sensible, I guess.\n\nI’ll have yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I may skip lunch without really thinking much of it, and then dinner is very measured so the day doesn’t sort of spread out all over the place. It reminds me of scheduling work—though, I suppose that’s not exactly the same thing, is it? But there’s a comfort in knowing what’s coming and not having to make too many decisions.\n\nSo yes, preventing weight gain is part of it, but the way it feels to me day-to-day is more like keeping everything in its proper place.\n\nDoctor: Is your strict meal planning something you think you could relax if you didn't have to worry about your weight at all?\n\nPatient: If I truly didn’t have to worry about my weight at all? Maybe I could relax it some, but I’m not sure I know how to separate the two.\n\nI mean, I came in because my doctor thinks my meal planning has gotten too tight, though to me it feels more like keeping things orderly. Yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I might just not stop for lunch—though that happens with paperwork too, you know, you look up and the afternoon’s gone—and then dinner is measured so the day doesn’t get away from me.\n\nSo I suppose if weight weren’t a worry, maybe the measuring would feel less necessary. But part of me would still want the structure. It’s hard to imagine just… eating without a plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your food intake if you were at a weight that you considered ideal?\n\nPatient: I think… probably yes, at least some. If I were at what I thought was the right weight, I’d still want the routine, because that’s what keeps the day from feeling loose around the edges.\n\nI know my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight, but to me it just feels practical—yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and then dinner is very measured. It’s almost like how I used to organize reports at work—everything in its place, nothing left dangling. Though I suppose food isn’t exactly a spreadsheet, is it?\n\nSo yes, even at an ideal weight, I think I’d still feel pulled to keep it controlled. Maybe not as nervously, but I’d still want it orderly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
